- **Now recommended:** Confirm the answer the build already landed on and the owner's own words imply: no override, ever; a subclass that cannot meet an inherited rule declares the inability, the cage engine prices the residual against that party's appetite band and picks the loosest cage that fits, and deny is the bottom rung reached by the £. That is written into .scratch/policy-composition/spec.md and is the only formulation consistent with both 'no exemptions' and 'always caged'. One thing to watch and say out loud: a per-party file of declared inabilities is structurally a ledger of things a party cannot do, and the owner banned ledgers in categorical terms. It survives the ban because it grants nothing and costs money, but the wording in the deck must never call it an allowance.
- Rationale: The owner never answered this — 'prototype what we now understand is needed' was an instruction to build, not a ruling — so strictly nothing was ratified here. What makes it re-ratify rather than re-grill is that his later statements answer it more clearly than a fresh grilling would. I should also be plain that my framing at the time ('strictest wins vs most-derived wins') was borrowed from OO textbooks and would have led somewhere wrong: it treats weakening as a permission question when the owner had already reframed it as a pricing question.

### P067 — Coverage figure: a percentage, a list of what was not looked at plus raw counts, or both

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. platform/computed-semver/coverage.py, the coverage block in evidence/*.json, the Renovate PR body renderer, .scratch/computed-semver/issues/04 and 23. Adding a percentage later is additive; the risk is behavioural, not structural.
- Then recommended: (b). Publish counts and an explicit not-looked-at list. Publish no percentage at all, because a percentage invites a threshold and a threshold invites tuning the corpus until it passes.
- Owner's words: 'walk me through it, show me something thats actually real not just a bullshit grafana or other made up dashboard that could be not real data' (~2026-07-20) and 'show the real things, kubectl, failures, renovate dashboards' (2026-07-23). A coverage percentage is the archetype of the fabricable green number he rejected.
- **Now recommended:** Stands. Note one tension the eco-system frame creates and do not solve it with a percentage: if publishers sell policy baselines into a marketplace, subscribers will want a comparable quality signal across publishers, and raw counts are not comparable. The comparable artefact should be the not-looked-at list with its stable ids (P075) — 'this publisher discloses 3 carried-over holes, none new' compares across publishers without ever being a number anyone can tune.
- Rationale: Right decision, and it is genuinely implemented — coverage.py has no percentage anywhere and says so in its own docstring. The process fault is not the content: P067 through P071 all carry the timestamp 2026-08-21T20:01 and all carry the same reply, 'Agree'. Five architectural decisions were settled by one word in one minute, which is precisely the fatigue signature the owner has named.

### P068 — Unit of coverage measurement: cells, full pairwise shapes, or both

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **uncertain** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. coverage.py's two counts and the coverage block in evidence/*.json, plus the one-sentence pairwise statement in the PR body renderer.
- Then recommended: Report both with different jobs: cells (each predicate against satisfied/violated/absent) as a completeness self-check where a gap is a defect, and the pairwise gap stated as 'we built N of M combinations, pairwise by design' rather than as a ratio against the four-million-entry whole space.
- Owner's words: The brief carries nothing on measurement units. The nearest standing rule is 'leave no stone unturned, this is a marathon not a sprint, i'm in no rush, but i do need depth and coverage' (2026-08-04), which is about research depth rather than metric design, so I will not claim it as endorsement.
- **Now recommended:** Keep it. The distinction is the useful part and it survived contact: a cell gap is a generator defect that fails the build, a pair gap is a stated design limit that never blocks. One correction for honesty — the ticket promises that unspanned variables appear in the not-looked-at list, but the cage dial never appears there at all because it is referenced only from a mutation expression, not from a predicate. It is genuinely measured, by cage_engine.py's Track 2 cage-spec comparison, which is a stronger mechanism than coverage. Say that in the evidence rather than leaving a reader to assume the dial was checked by coverage.
- Rationale: Low-stakes and correct. Reporting both was also the answer that required me to decide the least, which is worth naming — 'report both' is often the shape a recommendation takes when the recommender does not want to pick.

### P071 — Artefact and its readers: separate artefacts per reader, or one signed source with multiple views

- Theme: feeds-wargaming-and-marketplace · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small to add the fourth view, medium to reverse. Reversing to per-reader artefacts touches gate.py's emit path, the sign step, the PR body renderer and the release notes link, and reintroduces the drift-between-copies problem the single source exists to prevent.
- Then recommended: One source, three views. CI writes one signed evidence JSON, renders it to markdown in the Renovate PR body, and the release notes link the signed file. The PR body gets the design effort because ADR-0002 makes the reviewed PR the non-negotiable moment.
- Owner's words: 'we've developed a reference arch, and a platform but also a whole economic platform and model for risk feeds' (2026-08-19), and the owner's Bloomberg/Gartner framing of subscribable machine-readable feeds. A single signed machine-readable source with rendered views is exactly a feed with a human presentation layer.
- **Now recommended:** Re-ratify the structure and add a fourth reader the decision did not anticipate: the twin and the marketplace. The signed evidence JSON is already the right shape to be a subscribable feed artefact — it carries outcome, bump, movement, counts, generator version, corpus checksum, wall-clock, holes, limits and the per-institution matrix, and it is gitsign-bundled next to the release. Publish it as a feed an adopter or the twin can consume rather than as a CI by-product that only a PR reviewer ever reads. That single change moves this decision from a governance detail to a piece of the economic platform the owner says the story is about.
- Rationale: Correct, and under-ambitious in a way I did not see at the time. I optimised for the reviewer in the PR because ADR-0002 was in front of me, and I sized the artefact to a code-review moment rather than to a market. The evidence files in platform/computed-semver/evidence/ are already signed and versioned; nothing consumes them outside CI.

### P073 — Who may add an exclusion entry: a single-tier list, or two tiers with different authority

- Theme: no-exemptions-all-policy · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. The loader schema in coverage.py, coverage-exclusions.yaml's header contract, and the tier field on not-looked-at entries. Collapsing to one tier is a few lines of code and a large loss of integrity — it would let a human write 'proved' next to any inconvenient hole.
- Then recommended: Two tiers, and the gate decides which one an entry gets. Entries the gate can prove unreachable become proved exclusions; entries it cannot prove stay declared holes and print in the not-looked-at list every release, for ever. A human may declare a hole but may never promote one to proved.
- Owner's words: 'there must never be an exemption ledger EVER, this is a banned concept explicitly, everything is codified' (2026-08-20) and 'its never an exemption it'll be a informed caging based on risk appetite/threat intel' (2026-08-21). The rule that a human may declare but never promote is what keeps the exclusion file from becoming the granted-exemption mechanism he banned; the permanent printing is the cost that stands in for the price.
- **Now recommended:** Stands, and it is the decision that makes P069 survivable — present the two together, because ratifying P069 without this is ratifying an escape hatch. The implementation honours it literally: coverage-exclusions.yaml reads only identity, name, expression and reason, and its loader has no field a human could set to promote an entry, with proved exclusions computed by coverage.py's own static_proof from the expression text. Extend the same authority split to the composition side, where a party's declared inabilities are the analogous artefact: a party may declare what it cannot meet, and only the cage engine may price it.
- Rationale: The best decision in this batch and the one I would defend unprompted. It is also the one that most deserved a real conversation rather than being the second of four items answered by a single 'Agree' at 06:45 on 2026-08-22, because it is the load-bearing constraint under an otherwise dangerous mechanism.

### P074 — Evidence field set: open-ended optional fields, or a fixed nine-field set

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **uncertain** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. platform/computed-semver/gate.py's evidence writer, evidence/*.json and their gitsign bundles, the PR body renderer, .scratch/computed-semver/issues/04 and 29. Adding a schema version is additive; regenerating the three existing signed evidence files is the only real cost.
- Then recommended: Nine fixed fields, none optional and nothing else: declared bump, computed bump, per-policy movement, three entry counts, generator version, corpus checksum, wall-clock, not-looked-at list, derived limits with counts, per-institution matrix.
- Owner's words: Nothing in the brief speaks to schema design. The adjacent standing rule is 'Make sure that we're covering all of the features that we built in the work recently. the whole lot.' (2026-08-25), which favours a mandatory floor over optional fields, but it is about deck coverage, not evidence schemas, so I will not overclaim it.
- **Now recommended:** Keep the mandatory floor — none of the nine may be optional, and the gate must emit and sign the whole document even when it refuses, which the build does. Drop the 'nothing else' half, because it is already false: the shipped evidence carries fifteen top-level keys (outcome, declared, subject_dir, old_subject_dir and computed_bump beyond the nine), so the rule as written is a claim the artefact contradicts, and this estate's argument is that governance tools lie by asserting what they do not check. Replace it with a schema version field and an additive rule: fields may be added, never removed or made optional. Under the marketplace north star that is the difference between an internal file and a feed contract a subscriber can pin.
- Rationale: The floor is right and the closure clause was over-tight in a way that predictably broke within days of shipping. Worth noting that the same 'Agree' at 06:45 covered P072 through P075 — four schema and semantics decisions in one token — and that a false claim of the form 'nothing else' is exactly the class of overclaim the tracker had to correct on tickets 09, 10 and 17.

### P075 — Whether a coverage hole has a stable identity across releases

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. coverage.py's stable_id and normalize_expr, coverage-baseline.yaml, the not-looked-at block in evidence/*.json and the PR body renderer. Reversing loses the new/carried/closed delta, which is most of the decision's value.
- Then recommended: Give each not-looked-at entry a stable id derived from the expression identity rather than its position, so the PR body can show holes as carried over, new, or closed since the last release.
- Owner's words: 'walk me through it, show me something thats actually real not just a bullshit grafana or other made up dashboard' (~2026-07-20) and 'not allow scope to drop in this and prematurely declare things as done' (2026-08-05). A hole count without identity lets a new hole hide behind a closed one; the delta is the only form in which the disclosure is hard to game.
- **Now recommended:** Stands. The build does it properly — stable_id hashes the normalised expression text scoped by identity family and version-stripped policy name, so an unchanged rule keeps its id from 1.0.0 to 3.0.0 while a changed rule gets a new one, with new / carried_over / closed tracked against coverage-baseline.yaml. Extend the same idea outward: control claims, declared inabilities and governed-namespace gaps all need stable ids for the same reason, so an adopter can see whether a publisher's disclosure got worse between versions. That is the mechanism that would make a feeds marketplace auditable rather than merely subscribable.
- Rationale: Sound, and it is the piece that turns the whole coverage story from a snapshot into a trend — 'a new hole in a release that claims patch is a strong signal to the reviewer' is the line the deck should use. No convenience motive here; it was more work than the alternative.

### P076 — What the not-looked-at hole's stable id is derived from (hash of normalised expression text, scoped by identity family and version-stripped policy name)

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. platform/computed-semver/coverage.py (955 lines) id derivation, the not-looked-at rendering in cut-release-gate.py, and the evidence JSON field. No adopter-side change. Reversing would only churn one release's hole diff.
- Then recommended: Reject message text (embeds version literals) and list index (reorder invents holes); derive the id from a hash of the normalised expression text, scoped by identity family and policy name with the version stripped, per ticket 06's normalisation rule.
- Owner's words: No direct owner words on hole identity, but the surrounding rule is his: 'make sure the ticket isn't closed as its not done (or reopen it)' (2026-07-31) and the standing guard 'not allow scope to drop in this and prematurely declare things as done' (2026-08-05). A hole that silently reads as new at every release is exactly the honesty failure those two guards exist to prevent. He also asked for 'the real things, kubectl, failures' (2026-07-23) rather than clean surfaces.
- **Now recommended:** Keep the hash-of-normalised-expression id. One extension for the eco-system frame: the id is currently only stable within one publisher's repo. In a feeds marketplace where a regulator (ico, nist) and the platform each publish policy, an adopter comparing coverage across publishers needs the id namespaced by publishing party, so 'this hole is carried over' is answerable across the whole eco-system, not just inside one repo's history.
- Rationale: This is honest plumbing, not a framing choice. The two rejected options are genuinely broken (version literals in message text would have made every hole look new at exactly the release where holes matter). Nothing in the owner's evolution touches it. My candour note is only that it is small enough that a bare 'Agree' costs him nothing either way — this is one of the entries where fatigue and genuine assent are indistinguishable and it does not matter.

### P077 — Whether the release gate emits and signs evidence when it refuses

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small-medium if reversed. gate.py outcome handling, cut-release-gate.py, the signing step in platform/.github/workflows, and the adopter gates that read the outcome field. Reversing would delete the only durable record of every refusal to date.
- Then recommended: Always emit and always sign, on refusal as well as success; an 'outcome' field carries 'refused' and names the reason, every other field populated as far as the run got. A refusal is the most valuable evidence the gate produces.
- Owner's words: 'show the real things, kubectl, failures, renovate dashboards' (2026-07-23) and the rejection of 'a bullshit grafana or other made up dashboard that could be not real data' (~2026-07-20). Also 'a failure in ci is a big deal' (2026-07-23) — a failure he treats as significant must leave a record. The Docker incident, 'how on earth are you saying its working!?!!' (2026-08-25), is the same complaint about missing negative evidence.
- **Now recommended:** Keep, and go further against the eco-system north star: a signed refusal is the one artefact that proves a publisher tried to under-declare a break and was stopped. In a marketplace where adopter orgs and regulators subscribe to a publisher's feed, refusal evidence should be publishable to that feed, not only kept in the publisher's CI. That converts an internal build failure into a market signal about publisher quality — which is the 'economic platform and model for risk feeds' he named on 2026-08-19.
- Rationale: The strongest decision in the batch and the one most clearly on the owner's own line. It is also the opposite of assistant convenience: signing a failure path is more work than skipping it, and it creates artefacts that can embarrass the build. That is the tell that it was not optimised for the demo.

### P078 — Refuse, warn, or auto-correct a declared bump weaker than the computed one

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium. This is the gate's core verdict path: gate.py, cut-release-gate.py, platform/verify-publisher-gate.sh, and every release workflow in platform, nist, ico, driftwood, ludlow, tuppence. Reversing to warn-and-proceed would make the whole computed-semver epic advisory and hollow out tickets 18-30.
- Then recommended: Refuse, and never rewrite the tag. Permit a stronger declared bump with the discrepancy printed in evidence. Split the rule on the under/over-declare asymmetry.
- Owner's words: '22 whatever the best practice for implementing semvers says' (2026-08-22) — under-declaring a break is the one thing semver practice forbids outright. 'how much have we captured that policy versions should be semver and can have inheritence model themselves since they are code' (2026-08-20). The refuse-don't-rewrite half also matches 'war-gamer that opens signed PRs (propose-never-dispose)' (2026-07-23): machinery proposes, humans dispose.
- **Now recommended:** Keep refuse-never-rewrite. But rewrite the refusal message in the owner's current vocabulary: under his 2026-08-22 model, refusal is the degenerate cage where no satisfiable spec exists, not a gate saying no. The gate should say what it costs — 'this release can only ship as 3.0.0, or as 2.1.0 with the breaking expressions withdrawn' — so the publisher sees a priced choice rather than a wall. That is the 'informed caging based on risk appetite/threat intel' (2026-08-21) shape applied to the publisher instead of the workload.
- Rationale: The substance survives his evolution; only the framing is stale. I should be candid that 'refuse' was also the cheapest of the three to implement and the most demoable — a red X on stage — and that auto-correct was dismissed fast. But auto-correct genuinely breaks provenance (the tag would no longer be the human's stated intent), so the cheap answer and the right answer coincide here.

### P080 — One shared gate or two (publisher computes and signs; adopter verifies then composes its own bump)

- Theme: multi-org-ecosystem · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Large if reversed. Collapsing to one shared gate would delete all three adopter gates (~2.4k lines across driftwood, ludlow, tuppence), the identity-pinning work in P089, the shift-left wiring in ticket 28, and the whole cross-party composition story in the policy-composition epic. It would also make the multi-org split decorative, which is the thing he ordered done for real on 2026-08-19.
- Then recommended: Two gates asking different questions. The publisher computes and signs one bump at the strictest band; the adopter verifies the signature identity-pinned, then computes its own composed bump from its own pinned set.
- Owner's words: '2 split everything. We are demonstrating a multi org thing' (2026-08-19). 'the intent was never to inherit from tiself, it was to inherit from others and allow for policy like any other dpeendency to be a mash up like an object orienta[ted]' (2026-08-21) — cross-party composition is precisely what the adopter's own gate computes. Today's steer: 'a broader whole eco-system, with the orgs as an example consumers'.
- **Now recommended:** Keep, unchanged in shape. This is the decision the rest of the eco-system frame hangs on: it is what makes the adopter orgs consumers rather than branches of the publisher. If anything, extend it — the twin should be a third reader of the same signed evidence (intelligence, not enforcement), and a regulator should be able to verify a publisher's bump without being either party.
- Rationale: The most north-star-aligned decision in the batch, and one he independently reached from two directions (split everything, inherit from others). It was accepted with a bare 'Agree' but it is not a fatigue artefact — he had already argued this position himself twice in the preceding three days.

### P081 — Ship the gate hard, or with a grace mode, given five unversioned Kyverno policies block every release from day one

- Theme: no-exemptions-all-policy · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. Five policy files' version stamps and the gate's refusal condition. If reversed to a grace mode, the blast radius is conceptual rather than mechanical: it reintroduces the banned concept under a new name and every later 'this one is temporarily exempt' argument gets a precedent.
- Then recommended: Ship hard. Version the five unversioned policies first as a prerequisite task under ticket 07. A grace mode is a threshold that never gets removed.
- Owner's words: 'there must never be an exemption ledger EVER, this is a banned concept explicitly, everything is codified' (2026-08-20). 'nothing is a nice to have, you're either building it or your not' (2026-07-23). 'all of this is "the policy" we're not carving exemptions, or greater complexity' (2026-07-31). A grace mode is a time-boxed exemption with better manners, which is the concept he banned.
- **Now recommended:** Keep, unchanged. The only refinement the eco-system frame adds: 'ship hard' should mean hard for the platform too, not only for the policy body — which P087 already secures. Verify the five actually got versions rather than being quietly excluded via the P073 declared-hole path; that path is the grace mode's back door.
- Rationale: Right answer, and it cost the assistant work rather than saving it (a prerequisite task instead of a flag). Worth noting that the argument used to win the 'Agree' was his own 2026-08-20 ruling handed back to him — legitimate, but it means the 'Agree' carries less independent information than it looks like it does. The decision stands on its own merits regardless.

### P082 — Whether there is a legitimate override path for a human who believes the computed bump is wrong and wants a weaker one

- Theme: no-exemptions-all-policy · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small mechanically (there is nothing to remove — the override was never built), large in precedent. Reversing would require a new override artefact, which is the banned concept re-entering through the release path, and would undercut P081 and the whole govern-what-you-dont-control epic's cage framing.
- Then recommended: No override at all. An override is what exemptions.yaml carried and that argument lost on 2026-08-20. Make the refusal actionable through a reviewed PR to the generator or the policy instead.
- Owner's words: 'there must never be an exemption ledger EVER, this is a banned concept explicitly, everything is codified' (2026-08-20). 'its never an exemption it'll be a informed caging based on risk appetite/threat intel' (2026-08-21). 'Exemptions are less an exemption. They are the bau... if anyone in the org is able to meet the rules they can' (2026-07-23) — an override available to one release and not to all is exactly the shape he rejected.
- **Now recommended:** Keep no override, and complete it with the half his 2026-08-21 words demand. Today the refusal just says no. Under 'informed caging based on risk appetite/threat intel' the honest answer to 'I want a weaker bump' is not silence, it is a priced alternative: ship at the computed bump, or ship weaker with the affected expressions withdrawn, or ship into a tighter cage — with the £ consequence attached via the one-currency model. That makes 'no override' a codified conditional rather than a wall, which is his stated model.
- Rationale: Correct and squarely his position. The candid note is the same as P081: the recommendation won by citing his own ruling back at him, so the 'Agree' is nearly content-free as evidence. The decision is also, unusually, the harder path for the assistant — no override means no escape hatch when the gate misfires during a demo, which is a real risk he should know he accepted.

### P083 — Which predecessor the gate compares against (N-1 only, the whole supported window strictest-wins, or every tag ever cut)

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium. comparison_window.py (338 lines), the strictest-wins reduction in gate.py, the per-institution matrix in the evidence, and ticket 24. Reversing to N-1 would be a smaller, faster gate that silently stops protecting anyone not on the newest version — the failure would be invisible until an adopter broke.
- Then recommended: Every version in the supported window, strictest result wins. Comparing only N-1 hides breaks for clusters sitting on N-2.
- Owner's words: His own reply minutes later at P091: '20 just like with normal software, it may be desirable to patch a previous old version of the policy whilst still suppor[ting]' (2026-08-22) — a supported window is exactly what that presumes. '22 whatever the best practice for implementing semvers says' (2026-08-22). And 'kubectl-style +/-1 version skew, no surprises at deploy time' from the 2026-07-23 shift-left ambition, which only means anything if more than one live version is measured.
- **Now recommended:** Keep. In the eco-system frame this is also what lets an adopter org sit deliberately behind the head version without falling off the evidence — a consumer's right to lag is only real if the publisher is still measuring against where the consumer actually is. Worth checking the window's size is derived from real adopter pins rather than a constant.
- Rationale: This one is more expensive to implement than the alternative (N comparisons instead of one) and the assistant recommended it anyway, so it was not convenience-driven. The owner independently supplied the supporting principle within the same hour without being asked.

### P084 — How evidence is signed, transported and verified (existing feeds/sign.sh PEM key vs cosign sign-blob keyless)

- Theme: multi-org-ecosystem · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium. The signing step in platform release workflows, the verify path in all three adopter gates, verify-publisher-gate.sh, and the identity constants of P089. Converging feeds as well would additionally touch platform/feeds/verify.sh, platform/feeds/keys, and the ico and nist release workflows.
- Then recommended: Drop feeds/sign.sh; sign with cosign sign-blob keyless using the OIDC identity for both outcomes; commit the evidence JSON and bundle at a fixed path on success; emit artifact plus job summary on refusal.
- Owner's words: The 2026-07-23 spec line he wrote: 'gitsign-to-Rekor provenance' and 'every actor attestable'. 'per-org signing keys as a stopgap until Flux ships gitsign' (2026-08-19) — he treats PEM keys as the stopgap and keyless as the destination, which is exactly this direction of travel.
- **Now recommended:** Keep cosign keyless for evidence, but finish the job: the estate now runs two provenance schemes side by side. platform/feeds/keys plus ico/schema/sign.sh sign the feeds with PEM, while the semver evidence is signed keyless. For a feeds marketplace where a regulator's fine schedule and a publisher's release evidence are consumed by the same adopter, one verification story is worth more than two. Converge feeds onto keyless too, or state plainly why a regulator publishing outside GitHub cannot.
- Rationale: Right call for the evidence path and consistent with his stated destination. Two candour notes. First, 'drop feeds/sign.sh' was recommended and then not actually done — feeds still ship with PEM keys, so the decision was half-executed and the divergence it created is real, not theoretical. Second, the offline-verification argument that carried this and P090 rests on a constraint he had killed on 2026-08-19 ('5 false constraint. Assume internet always').

### P087 — Whether an array-only platform release (retirement, no policy body change) triggers the gate, and whether retirement computes major

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small-medium. The array-only trigger condition and the retirement rule in gate.py and comparison_window.py, ticket 15's repair release which exercised it, and the interaction with P091's before-window comparison. Reversing would let a platform release quietly drop a version an adopter is pinned to, at a patch bump.
- Then recommended: The gate runs on an array-only release and a retired element computes major. Don't wait for ticket 07, which only owns the numbering mechanics.
- Owner's words: '22 whatever the best practice for implementing semvers says' (2026-08-22) — removing a supported version is a breaking change under any reading of semver. '20 just like with normal software, it may be desirable to patch a previous old version of the policy whilst still suppor[ting]' (2026-08-22) presumes retirement is a real, consequential event. The map's own destination line, 'The distribution layer does not get to exempt itself from the rule it distributes', is the no-exemptions principle applied to the platform.
- **Now recommended:** Keep. In the eco-system frame retirement is the most consumer-visible act a publisher performs — it is the moment an adopter org discovers its pinned version has no support — so major is the honest signal and it should also be announced on the publisher's feed, not only encoded in a version number that adopters may not read until Renovate wakes.
- Rationale: Substantively right. The one candour note is on the 'don't wait for ticket 07' half: that was partly ticket-flow convenience — it let this ticket close without a dependency — and it did leave two tickets touching the same platform-versioning surface. The technical judgement underneath is still correct and ticket 07 was not in fact contradicted.

### P088 — Which ref the cross-org adopter gate checks out — it was checking out platform's default branch, not the institution's pinned tag (live bug)

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. The checkout step and the commit-match assertion in the three adopter gates and shift-left.yml. Reversing reinstates a gate that verifies whatever is on platform's default branch, i.e. a green check that means nothing — worse than no gate.
- Then recommended: Check out the tag under review, read from gitops/platform/platform-pin.yaml at PR head, verify the resolved commit matches the pinned commit and refuse otherwise. Fix inside this ticket rather than logging it as future work.
- Owner's words: 'make sure the ticket isn't closed as its not done (or reopen it)' (2026-07-31). 'goes in all the repos its supposed to go in' (2026-08-25). 'how on earth are you saying its working!?!!' (2026-08-25) — a gate that verified the wrong artefact is precisely the class of false green he lost his temper over. And 'a failure in ci is a big deal, a deploy time fail even to a dev env is a really big deal' (2026-07-23).
- **Now recommended:** Keep, and treat the commit-match check as the general rule rather than one gate's fix: any cross-org checkout in this eco-system must resolve to the pinned commit and refuse on mismatch. This is a known repeat offender in the estate — shallow clones, pull_request merge-commit checkouts and cross-repo default refs have each cost a live CI round-trip — so a shared, reused checkout step is worth more than three copies of the same guard.
- Rationale: A real defect found and fixed rather than filed, which is the behaviour he has asked for repeatedly. The bare 'Agree' here was assent to fixing a bug, not to a design position, so fatigue does not really put it in doubt. Only note against my own side: the bug existed at all because the adopter gate was wired before anyone checked which ref it read.

### P089 — Where the adopter's expected-identity constant lives (fetched from platform vs each institution holds its own pinned copy)

- Theme: identity-posture-eud · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small now, medium later. Three pinned constants plus the verify paths in driftwood/scripts/verify-identity-regexp.sh, tuppence/scripts/verify-identity-regexp.sh and ludlow/verify-certificate-identity-regexp.sh, and ticket 14's anchored regexp work. Reversing would collapse the trust model — the checked party would supply its own trust root — and hollow out the multi-org demonstration.
- Then recommended: Each institution holds its own copy as a pinned constant, changed only by a reviewed PR. Never fetch a trust identity from the party being checked.
- Owner's words: 'so we also need to think about the policy being a baked in identity attribute for the running services, so that its an attestation/property on the service accou[nt]' (2026-07-31), taken 'under least privilege and zero trust'. '2 split everything. We are demonstrating a multi org thing' (2026-08-19). Fetching the trust root from the signer is the one move that would make the multi-org split cosmetic.
- **Now recommended:** Keep. Name the cost while re-ratifying: with one publisher and three adopters, per-institution pinned constants are trivial; in the marketplace he is aiming at — multiple publishers, regulators, and adopter orgs — rotation becomes N publishers by M consumers, each needing a reviewed PR. The answer is not to fetch from the publisher; it is that a rotation eventually needs a distribution mechanism that is not the party being checked. Worth naming now and solving when the publisher count grows, not before.
- Rationale: Correct on zero-trust grounds and independently supported by his 2026-07-31 identity thinking. It was also the less convenient option — a fetched constant would have been one line and no per-repo duplication — so this was not an assistant-convenience call.

### P090 — Where the cosign verification bundle lives (PR comment only vs committed next to the evidence JSON)

- Theme: multi-org-ecosystem · Alignment: **drifted** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. A committed path in the release commit, the bundle read in the three adopter gates, and the PR-comment rendering in ticket 29. Reversing to PR-comment-only would make every historical verification depend on GitHub's comment retention, which is a worse dependency than the one the original rationale claimed to avoid.
- Then recommended: Commit the bundle next to the evidence JSON in the release commit so verification is offline by construction; keep PR-comment rendering as a convenience view.
- Owner's words: The decision's stated justification is one he had explicitly killed three days earlier: '5 false constraint. Assume internet always' (2026-08-19). The decision itself is still supported by other words of his — 'gitsign-to-Rekor provenance' and 'every actor attestable' (2026-07-23) — but not by the reason he was given when he typed 'Agree'.
- **Now recommended:** Keep the committed bundle, on the correct grounds. The reason is not offline operation, it is durability and jurisdiction: a regulator or adopter re-verifying a release two years on needs the bundle in the release commit and not dependent on a third-party transparency log's availability or retention. That reason holds in the eco-system frame; the offline one does not, and should be corrected wherever it is written down.
- Rationale: The right outcome reached by a dead argument, which is the clearest example in this batch of what the owner is worried about. He typed 'Agree' to a rationale he had personally rejected 72 hours earlier — evidence that at least some of these agrees were not read closely. The decision survives on replacement grounds, but the record should not keep citing offline verification as why.

### P091 — Which window a retiring release compares against

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small-to-medium. comparison_window.py in platform/computed-semver, the gate's retirement path, the evidence schema, and the classification recorded for the real repair release. Reversing silently re-classifies past retirements as patches and invalidates signed evidence for the release that retired policy/v1.0.0.
- Then recommended: A retiring release compares against the supported window as it stood BEFORE the release, so a retirement falls out as major with no special case (the pinned consumer loses its pin).
- Owner's words: The recorded reply is not actually about retirement at all: `20 just like with normal software, it may be desirable to patch a previous old version of the policy whilst still supporting` — that answers backports (P092/P093), and the retirement question was swept up in the same bundled agreement. Supporting owner material is indirect: Window A brief prose, 'he wants ... sunset times on policy versions' (2026-07-16), and 2026-08-25 `goes in all the repos its supposed to go in`. There is no verbatim owner line on the comparison window.
- **Now recommended:** Keep the before-window rule, and under the eco-system north star make retirement a published market event, not just a bump class: the evidence file for a retiring release should name the consumers losing their pin and carry a sunset date, because in a feeds marketplace a publisher retiring a version is the most expensive thing it can do to its subscribers.
- Rationale: Mechanically it is the only rule that makes retirement visible without a special case; comparing against the post-retirement window measures nothing because the retired element is gone from both sides. But be honest about the record: this was logged as agreement on the strength of a reply about backports. If anything re-opens here it is the sunset/notice shape, not the comparison direction.

### P092 — What a backport compares against

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium. comparison_window.py, gate.py predecessor selection, every signed evidence file, and the classification of the shipped policy/v2.0.1 backport. Reversing re-opens the N-2 blind spot and re-classifies the estate's only real backport.
- Then recommended: Compare against every supported version LOWER than the declared version, strictest result wins; unchanged for a head release, narrowed to the line below for a backport.
- Owner's words: `20 just like with normal software, it may be desirable to patch a previous old version of the policy whilst still supporting` and `22 whatever the best practice for implementing semvers says / Otherwise, I agree` (2026-08-22). Window D constraint: 'Semver mechanics follow published best practice, not local invention.'
- **Now recommended:** Keep it. Add one honest caveat to the spec: semver 2.0.0 has no opinion on multi-version comparison windows, so this rule is a local invention that 'whatever best practice says' does not actually cover. Publish it as part of the eco-system contract adopters read, rather than leaving it implicit in gate code.
- Rationale: Right answer, wrong warrant. Comparing only against N-1 hides a break for a cluster on N-2, which multi-version coexistence guarantees exists. My side of it: 'whatever best practice says' was read as blanket assent to a rule the specification is silent on — the exact fatigue-agreement pattern this audit exists to catch. The rule earned its keep later, when proving the real backport surfaced a wrong-predecessor bug in gate_one().

### P093 — Maintenance branches break the gitsign identity pin

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium-large. cut-release.yml and release.yml in platform, the EXPECTED_IDENTITY constant held by each of the three institutions, and verification of the published policy/v2.0.1 tag. Reversing makes an already-adopted signed release fail verification at every adopter.
- Then recommended: Adopt release/<major>.<minor>.x branches and pin with an anchored --certificate-identity-regexp allowing refs/heads/main and that branch shape; do it in this ticket because backports are otherwise unreleasable.
- Owner's words: `20 just like with normal software, it may be desirable to patch a previous old version of the policy whilst still supporting` (backports must be possible) and `22 whatever the best practice for implementing semvers says` (2026-08-22). Also the 2026-07-23 provenance requirement, 'gitsign-to-Rekor provenance', and Window D 'per-org signing keys as a stopgap until Flux ships gitsign'.
- **Now recommended:** Keep it, and treat the identity regexp as a first-class published artefact of the eco-system: it is the trust contract an adopter (later a regulator or feeds subscriber) uses to decide a signed release is really from this publisher. Keep it anchored, and never fetch it from the publisher being checked (see P089).
- Rationale: Without it the backport line is unreleasable and P091/P092 are theory. It shipped for real: policy/v2.0.1 cut from release/2.0.x and gitsign-verified. The loosening is narrow — ref only; org, repo and workflow path stay pinned — so provenance is not weakened the way a wildcard identity would weaken it.

### P095 — Q0 - is 'unversioned' the right frame, or is the real defect unpinned delivery

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Large, already spent. distribution/versions.yaml, all four version trees, the renderer, up.sh demoted to an offline twin, the gate corpus, and the three institutions' pins. Reversing means accepting policy that reaches clusters outside the pinned path, unwinding the repair release.
- Then recommended: (a) This ticket owns delivery AND numbering together, because four of the five unversioned policies reach a cluster via kubectl apply -f in up.sh rather than the pinned path; raise the installed-set assumption back to cs-05 as a finding.
- Owner's words: Reply `0 agree`. Independent owner words that carry it: option (c) was to declare graded/posture demo scaffolding and shrink the claim, which `nothing is a nice to have, you're either building it or your not` (2026-07-23) and `build everything` (2026-07-31) forbid. Also `goes in all the repos its supposed to go in` (2026-08-25) and `flux is a integral part/enabler to this (unless we prove otherwise)` (2026-08-05) — policy delivered by a shell script is not Flux-borne.
- **Now recommended:** Keep (a), and state the invariant at eco-system level: nothing enforces in a cluster unless it arrived by the pinned, signed path. Where several publishers ship policy, a second delivery path is not untidiness, it is an unprovenanced control no subscriber can verify.
- Rationale: Correct, and the most valuable finding in the cs series. Candour on my side: the delivery hole was created by earlier assistant work reported as done, and the ticket that found it also absorbed the blame. Option (c) — shrink the claim to demo scaffolding — is the scope trim the owner has banned twice, and it was the cheapest option for me.

### P097 — Q5 - how the anti-forgery pair survives one version mechanism

- Theme: identity-posture-eud · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium. The version-tree renderer, all four committed trees, and the gate's mandatory-member list (which doubles as the enforcement-surface list for rule 7.3). Reversing re-opens a silent hole in which a version ships forgeable posture.
- Then recommended: (b) A renderer generates the mandatory members (stamp-posture, posture-trust-boundary) into every version tree automatically, so a human cannot omit one; remove the failure rather than detect it.
- Owner's words: `nothing runs without a cage even if they're permissive` (2026-08-20) and `you’re always caged even if it’s a permissive one` — a version tree shipped without its anti-forgery pair is a pod running uncaged. Also 2026-07-31: policy as `a baked in identity attribute for the running services, so that its an attestation/property on the service accou[nt]` — the pair is what stops that attribute being forged.
- **Now recommended:** Keep it, paired with the refusal (cs-07 rule 7.3) so the invariant is enforced twice: generated in, and refused if absent. Under the eco-system frame the anti-forgery pair is what makes a posture claim tradeable between orgs, so it belongs in a published mandatory-member contract, not a platform-local render detail.
- Rationale: Generating the invariant beats reviewing for it, and this is one of the few decisions in the batch where the mechanism removes a class of failure rather than reporting it. For the record, the owner had already overruled me on the parent question — I argued the pair should sit outside the version mechanism, the owner chose one mechanism, and this decision repairs the hole that choice opened.

### P098 — Q6 - do the cage's non-Kyverno dials (PriorityClasses) enter the version trees

- Theme: cages-and-graded-enforcement · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium. priorityclasses.yaml in each version tree, cage-tier's inline dial map (which selects by versioned name), the renderer, and every cluster's cluster-scoped object set. Reversing re-shares one PriorityClass set across versions, breaking per-version cage immutability for already-pinned pods.
- Then recommended: (a) Yes, versioned copies with versioned names (cage-baseline-1-0-0 etc.), recommended 'reluctantly, as the price of the Q1 answer' — three cluster-scoped objects per installed version, nine per cluster at the coexistence floor.
- Owner's words: `you’re always caged even if it’s a permissive one. It’s the spec of the cage that can change` (2026-08-20). Eviction priority is part of the cage spec: it decides which pod the kubelet reclaims first. No owner words exist on the object-count cost.
- **Now recommended:** Keep it. A pod pinned to 2.0.0 must keep 2.0.0's eviction behaviour for ever, and a shared PriorityClass edited once reaching every version at the same instant is exactly the unversioned-mutable-dial defect this epic exists to close. Do surface the cluster-scoped object growth (3 x installed versions) as a named operating cost, because nobody priced it with the owner.
- Rationale: Consistency here is not aesthetic: option (b) would have left the sharpest dial in the cage outside the version boundary while claiming the cage is versioned. My reluctance in the original recommendation was about duplication, a cost I bear rather than the estate bears. The honest gap is that a bare 'agree' bought a permanent cluster-scoped name explosion the owner was never shown a number for.

### P099 — Q7 - what the platform's own number covers, and what computes it

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Large. gate.py, the corpus and generated-corpus, coverage accounting, cut-release.yml (three tags from one commit), and the platform pins at all three institutions. Collapsing or splitting namespaces now would invalidate every signed evidence file and every adopter pin.
- Then recommended: (a) One rule, one gate, one corpus: the platform tag is computed by the same gate against the same corpus and is major when a platform change can move any workload's cage spec or admission; keep the two tag namespaces rather than collapsing them.
- Owner's words: `there must never be an exemption ledger EVER, this is a banned concept explicitly, everything is codified` (2026-08-20) and `all of this is 'the policy' we're not carving exemptions, or greater complexity` (2026-07-31) — exempting the distribution layer from the rule it enforces is the self-exemption those lines ban. Two namespaces are required by multi-version coexistence, held since 2026-07-23.
- **Now recommended:** Keep it, and lift it out of platform. Under the eco-system north star this rule is the product: a publisher (nist, ico, a feeds vendor) that wants its releases consumable runs the same computation against its own corpus and publishes signed evidence. Today it is platform-private machinery; it should be a specified, adoptable gate — that is what makes a marketplace rather than one clever repo.
- Rationale: The anti-self-exemption argument is the owner's own and independent of the fatigue. One caveat I should own: 'one rule, one gate, one corpus' was also the cheapest thing to build and the easiest ticket to close, and it happened to be right. The reply was one of four bare 'Agree's inside the same minute (10:31), so treat the record's confidence as low even where the answer holds.

### P100 — Q8 - does the platform number cover the orphan guard's own CEL

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small-medium. The versions.yaml template, the orphan-guard renderer and its offline twin, cs-06's identity rule, and the platform tag's classification. Reversing exempts the one file that writes a Deny from measurement.
- Then recommended: (a) The platform number covers the versions.yaml template as well as the array, classified by the same verdict rule; do not treat the template as exempt machinery.
- Owner's words: `there must never be an exemption ledger EVER, this is a banned concept explicitly, everything is codified` (2026-08-20); the template is where a Deny is written, so calling it machinery is a self-exemption. Also `all of this is 'the policy'` (2026-07-31).
- **Now recommended:** Keep it. Note the tension it leaves: the answer document then grants the rendered orphan guard the identity family `platform-machinery` so cs-06's labelling rule does not fail on it. That is a class-level carve-out one paragraph from a decision that refused a carve-out. It is defensible (an aggregate over the array cannot self-scope to one claim) but it should be written up as an explicit, measured exception with its own version line, not a family name that quietly excuses membership.
- Rationale: The decision itself matches the owner's hardest constraint. The carve-out that followed is the part worth watching, and I never put it to the owner as a decision at all — it arrived inside the answer prose after the agreement.

### P101 — Q9 - what happens to the orphaned policy/policies/v1.0.0 tree

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium, already spent. The deleted policy/policies/ tree, the published policy/v2.0.1 tag, the version array and its resolved commits. Reversing (a second family) needs a second claim label and selector, with changes reaching the compose/ work and all three adopters.
- Then recommended: (b) Fold it into the single distribution line at 1.0.1 as a backport (its rule nonroot || (attested && hardened) is strictly wider, and a widening is a patch); reject making it a second policy family with its own claim.
- Owner's words: `20 just like with normal software, it may be desirable to patch a previous old version of the policy whilst still supporting` (2026-08-22) supports the backport shape. But the stated reason for rejecting a second family — that the one-string pin doubling as the workload selector is 'the original's signature elegance' — has no owner backing and cuts across `the intent was never to inherit from tiself, it was to inherit from others and allow for policy like any other dpeendency to be a mash up like an object orienta[tion]` (2026-08-21).
- **Now recommended:** Keep the fold — two trees both claiming 1.0.0 with contradictory rules is a live bug and had to die. But separate the two things decided together: deleting a contradictory orphan tree is obviously right; concluding that a workload carries exactly one claim string from one family is a real constraint on cross-party composition, justified on elegance. Under the eco-system north star a workload will need to claim against several publishers at once (a regulator line, a customer SLA line, a platform line), so that premise must not be treated as settled by this ticket.
- Rationale: Right outcome, and it shipped (as policy/v2.0.1 from release/2.0.x after the repair release renumbered the line). The candour point is the warrant: 'signature elegance' is a narrative argument — the demo-shaped reasoning the owner has rejected before — and it should never have locked in a composition constraint on a bare 'Agree'.

### P102 — Q10 - does the platform leave 0.x

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small in principle, large to unwind. The platform tag line and every institution's platform-pin.yaml. Going back to 0.x strips the gate of authority over the repairs it was built to police.
- Then recommended: (a) Cut platform 1.0.0 as part of the repair release, because semver 2.0.0 gives 0.x no compatibility guarantee and the gate would have nothing to refuse for exactly the period these repairs land; refuse to invent a local 0.x dialect.
- Owner's words: `22 whatever the best practice for implementing semvers says` (2026-08-22) — semver 2.0.0 is explicit that 0.x carries no guarantee, so applying >=1.0.0 rules at 0.x by local rule is precisely the local invention that instruction forbids.
- **Now recommended:** Keep it, unchanged. It is the cleanest owner-word match in the batch and the one that makes the publisher credible to a subscriber: a policy/feeds publisher sitting at 0.x is telling the market its versions mean nothing.
- Rationale: Derived from a real owner instruction rather than an assistant preference, and it shipped (platform v1.0.0, now v1.1.1). Nothing in the eco-system pivot changes it.

### P103 — Q12 - where the source of truth lives, and what git holds

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **uncertain** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium. The renderer, the four committed version trees, cs-06's YAML parsing, and every verify beat. Reversing to reconcile-time templates blinds the gate; reversing to hand-authored copies re-introduces the omission failure P097 removed.
- Then recommended: (2) One authoring copy under graded/ and posture/, a renderer emits per-version copies, and the emitted copies are committed; not hand-authored N copies, not rendered at reconcile time.
- Owner's words: No owner statement on rendering strategy exists in the brief. The reply was `Agree` at 10:31, one of four inside that minute. Nearest owner-anchored constraints are indirect: `flux is a integral part/enabler to this (unless we prove otherwise)` (2026-08-05) and the standing demand for real inspectable artefacts, `show the real things, kubectl, failures` (2026-07-23) — committed files are inspectable, a reconcile-time template is not.
- **Now recommended:** Keep it on merit, not on the recorded agreement. Committed rendered copies are what let the gate parse real YAML, let an adopter diff a tag offline, and put the enforcement surface inside the signed tag — all three serve the provenance leg of the north star. Reconcile-time rendering would hide every claim-wide policy from the gate, defeating the epic.
- Rationale: Sound, and the estate already owned the authoring-copy-plus-offline-twin pattern (render-orphan-guard.py), so it is reuse rather than invention. Be clear the owner never engaged with this one: a build-shape decision rubber-stamped in a batch, which should be presented as the assistant's call the owner may simply accept.

### P104 — Q15 - which trees CI re-renders, and what the gate reads for an older version

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **uncertain** · Blast radius if reversed: Small-medium. cut-release.yml, the gate's tag-reading path, and release_integrity.py / the frozen-tree check. Reversing to HEAD-reads lets a hand-edit change what a released version is claimed to mean, invalidating evidence for every past release.
- Then recommended: (a) Re-render only the tree being cut; read prior versions from their tags, never from HEAD; add a frozen-tree check that fails when a HEAD copy of a released tree differs from its tag.
- Owner's words: No owner words on re-render scope. Adjacent owner material supports the immutability half: `gitsign-to-Rekor provenance` and 'attestable provenance on every machine-authored commit' (2026-07-23). The reply was `Agree` at 10:41, in the same minute as P105.
- **Now recommended:** Keep it. The frozen-tree check is the load-bearing half and should be stated as an eco-system rule, not a CI convenience: a released version means what its signed tag says, and HEAD has no authority over it. Option (b), re-render everything and fail on any diff, would have frozen the dial table for ever — the opposite of what versioning is for.
- Rationale: Correct, and it turns cs-05's phrase 'the window as it stood before this release' from aspiration into mechanism. Candid note: (a) was also by far the cheapest to implement, so the recommendation and my convenience pointed the same way; the frozen-tree check is what stops that being a shortcut.

### P105 — Q16 - how the repair avoids uncaging every pod on an already-released version

- Theme: cages-and-graded-enforcement · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Large, already spent. The repair release itself (one commit, three signed tags), distribution/versions.yaml, all version trees, and the adopter PRs at driftwood, ludlow and tuppence. Reversing means accepting a window in which pinned pods run uncaged, contradicting the owner's stated absolute.
- Then recommended: (a) Re-cut both supported versions as new releases carrying the full policy set and swap the array elements in one release, so no cluster ever runs a version without a cage; reject side-by-side transition and reject retiring the old versions.
- Owner's words: `nothing runs without a cage even if they're permissive` (2026-08-20) and `you’re always caged even if it’s a permissive one. It’s the spec of the cage that can change` (2026-08-20). Also `3 there is no real gate anymore. Just cages` (2026-08-22) — if the cage is the enforcement, a day with no cage is a day with no enforcement.
- **Now recommended:** Keep it, and generalise the invariant beyond this one repair: no release may reduce the set of installed enforcement surfaces for any already-pinned version, at any instant, including transiently. That is the publisher-side promise a subscriber in the eco-system is actually buying.
- Rationale: The strongest owner-word match in the batch and the one decision here that directly protects the owner's own absolute. Side-by-side was genuinely unsafe (a shared cage-tier and a per-version cage-tier both stamp cpu on the same pod). It shipped, and proving it out earned its keep: the gate caught that the planned 1.0.2/2.0.1 numbers under-declared a real break, and the release was renumbered to an honest 2.0.0/3.0.0.

### P107 — Restating the major-bump rule at spec level for cage-tier's full mutation set

- Theme: cages-and-graded-enforcement · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small-to-medium. Cage-spec comparison in .scratch/computed-semver/issues/21-cage-spec-comparison-and-computed-bump.md, the gate's bump computation, ADR-0011's wording, and the recomputed history of any already-cut tags.
- Then recommended: (a) compare rendered cage specs; a release is major when the new spec is not at least as permissive as the old - stated at spec level, not enumerated per surface.
- Owner's words: Owner, 2026-08-22: '22 whatever the best practice for implementing semvers says' - and consumer-visible breakage is the semver definition of major, which is what 'not at least as permissive' encodes. Owner, 2026-08-20: 'you're always caged even if it's a permissive one. It's the spec of the cage that can change' - comparing rendered cage specs speaks in exactly the owner's own unit.
- **Now recommended:** Stands. One gap to close rather than reopen: 'at least as permissive' is a partial order, and cage changes are routinely incomparable (drop a capability, raise a CPU limit). Make the incomparable case explicitly major rather than leaving it to the comparison function's accident, and say so in ADR-0011.
- Rationale: The alternative (b) - enumerating counted surfaces - is the option that ages badly: every new cage surface silently escapes the rule until someone remembers to add it, which is the same class of hole as the orphaned claim the estate already guards against. (c) makes every cosmetic re-render a major. (a) is the right answer and is not one I picked for convenience; it is the more expensive one to implement.

### P110 — Where the computed-semver gate's tests attach

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. platform/computed-semver/ evidence module and its self-check; ticket 18 (the seam and version legality) and ticket 23 (coverage as counts and holes).
- Then recommended: One seam: a single evidence document in platform/computed-semver/ that returns computed bump, per-institution matrix, coverage counts, the not-looked-at list, derived limits and refusal reasons.
- Owner's words: Owner, 2026-07-17: 'so everything is 100% done, do a parallel workflow to determine that as true'; 2026-07-23: 'show the real things, kubectl, failures, renovate dashboards'. A single evidence document that must state its own coverage and name what it did not look at is the honesty artefact those demand. Mild tension with 2026-08-05: 'err on the side of splitting things up and introduce guardrails and defensive integration tests to make sure that things are coherent', which leans toward more seams rather than one.
- **Now recommended:** Stands, with one addition the eco-system framing demands: the evidence document is the thing a regulator or an adopter would actually consume, so it should be a signed, versioned output of the gate rather than a test fixture. It already carries the not-looked-at list - that is the most valuable thing in this whole map and it should be publishable.
- Rationale: A single seam was also the convenient answer - one place to write tests - and I should own that. But it is the right one here for a non-convenience reason: coverage stated rather than implied only works if there is exactly one document that has to state it. Standing caveat: the honesty gate currently covers 28 of 56 scripts, so the seam's discipline is not yet estate-wide.

### P111 — Ticket breakdown approval for computed-semver - granularity, blocking edges, splits, numbering

- Theme: process-and-tooling · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Large mechanically, zero architecturally. Tickets 12-30 and their filenames, map.md, spec.md, ADR-0011's ticket references, and the commit messages of every landed slice would all need renumbering. Nothing about the system's behaviour would change.
- Then recommended: Keep A4 whole; give Lane B no dependency on Lane A; number new tickets from 12 and leave 09-11 as split markers.
- Owner's words: Owner, 2026-08-04: 'consider factoring in disconnected elements that can run in parallel independently with their own checkpoints and be really explicit with acceptance criteria' - two independent lanes with their own acceptance criteria is a direct execution of that instruction. Owner, 2026-08-05: 'err on the side of splitting things up' - nineteen tickets from three is on the right side of that.
- **Now recommended:** Stands. Pure delivery mechanics with no architectural content, and it did what the owner asked of work decomposition.
- Rationale: The only self-interested element is the numbering choice - numbering from 12 avoided rewriting three ticket files and their inbound links, which suited me. It also preserved the audit trail, which is the better reason and the one that actually holds.

### P114 — Numbering the repair release once the gate computed the planned numbers as under-declaring a real break

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium-to-large and messy. Real signed tags v1.0.0, policy/v2.0.0 and policy/v3.0.0 are pushed and gitsign-verified; reversing means re-tagging published, signed history, plus versions.yaml pins in all three adopters, the Renovate PR bodies (ticket 29), the backport line (ticket 16) and the evidence documents that cite the numbers.
- Then recommended: None of the three options was marked Recommended. What was built: option 1 - renumbered to an honest major (platform v1.0.0, policy/v2.0.0, policy/v3.0.0), real gitsign-verified tags.
- Owner's words: Owner, 2026-08-22: '22 whatever the best practice for implementing semvers says'. And option 2 - shipping the planned numbers 'with a documented exception' - is barred outright by the owner's absolute of 2026-08-20: 'there must never be an exemption ledger EVER, this is a banned concept explicitly, everything is codified', reinforced 2026-08-21: 'its never an exemption it'll be a informed caging based on risk appetite/threat intel'. Only option 1 survives that.
- **Now recommended:** Stands, and it is the strongest moment in this batch: the gate refused its own author's plan and the number changed as a result. That is the demo beat - the tooling out-ranking the human's intention - and it should be narrated as such rather than buried in a ticket comment.
- Rationale: I did not recommend here, and the outcome was better for it: investigating the premise beat picking an option. The one honest caveat is that the release was hand-classified because the gate could not ship before the repair it enables - so the first application of the rule was a human applying it to himself.

### P115 — Is a composed policy artefact published and signed, or purely ephemeral

- Theme: multi-org-ecosystem · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium. ADR-0012, CONTEXT.md's Composed artefact term, policy-composition tickets 02/12/18, compose-check, the adopter CI signing step, and the evidence backfill landed in ticket 18.
- Then recommended: Published and signed - the composed set becomes a real artefact of its own, committed, versioned and signed, so a verifier can trust it without re-deriving it.
- Owner's words: Owner, 2026-08-21: 'the intent was never to inherit from tiself, it was to inherit from others and allow for policy like any other dpeendency to be a mash up like an object oriental[ted]' - a published build output is how dependency mash-ups behave. Owner, 2026-07-23, on provenance as a hard rule: 'gitsign-to-Rekor provenance' on every machine-authored change, and 'Nothing is a nice-to-have - all built.' Ephemeral composition is the cheaper option and would have left the composed result unattestable.
- **Now recommended:** Stands, and it is load-bearing for the eco-system north star specifically: publishing the composed set is what turns an adopter org from a consumer into a publisher, which is the mechanism by which the estate demonstrates a marketplace rather than a hierarchy. Say that out loud in ADR-0012 - right now it reads as a verifier convenience.
- Rationale: This was my recommendation and the more expensive of the two options, so it is not a convenience call. The residual cost is real and should be named: a new artefact class means a new thing to version, sign, garbage-collect and keep from going stale relative to its parents.

### P116 — Who holds the signing identity for a composed artefact

- Theme: multi-org-ecosystem · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small-to-medium. Adopter CI signing step in each of the three enactment repos, ADR-0012, and the verifier in policy-composition ticket 12.
- Then recommended: The adopter (the subclass) signs its own composed set, using the gitsign-signed tag mechanism it already uses - no second signing mechanism, no shared composition service.
- Owner's words: Owner, 2026-08-19: '2 split everything. We are demonstrating a multi org thing' - a shared composition service would re-centralise exactly what he ordered split. The same window's stopgap of per-org signing keys until Flux ships gitsign presumes each org signs its own output. The risk-bearer logic is the owner's own, restated in P120: the party that pays when it is wrong is the party that declares.
- **Now recommended:** Stands. Add the corollary that follows from the eco-system framing and is not yet written down: because the adopter signs, the adopter's signature is a claim about the composition, not about the parents - a verifier must still walk to each parent's own signature. ADR-0012 point 5's composed marker is what makes that walkable, so 0012 should state the two-step verification explicitly.
- Rationale: Reusing the existing gitsign tag path rather than inventing a signing service is the lazy answer and also the correct one - the alternative introduces a central trusted party into an architecture whose whole thesis is that there isn't one.

### P117 — Reproducibility bar for verifying a composed set

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **uncertain** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. compose.py determinism, compose-check in CI, ADR-0012 point 7, policy-composition ticket 12. Adding renderer pinning is additive rather than a reversal.
- Then recommended: Byte-for-byte deterministic re-render from the parents' pinned SHAs, rather than semantic equivalence - the bar the ticket 01 prototype already met.
- Owner's words: The brief contains no owner statement on determinism or on reproducibility bars, so I will not manufacture one. The nearest standing material is his verification posture - 2026-07-17: 'so everything is 100% done, do a parallel workflow to determine that as true' - and existing project doctrine in ADR-0006 (deterministic policy, no time conditions), neither of which is the owner speaking on this question.
- **Now recommended:** Keep byte-for-byte, but close the hole it creates in a multi-publisher world: byte-identity is a property of the renderer, not of the policy, so a renderer upgrade breaks every verifier at once and parties will not run identical toolchains. Make the composition renderer's own version a declared, pinned input of the composed artefact, verified alongside the parent SHAs.
- Rationale: Byte-for-byte was free here (the prototype already achieved it) and is strictly stronger, so it costs nothing to keep. Semantic equivalence would require defining semantic equality over Kyverno CEL policies, which is a research project nobody asked for. The renderer-pinning gap is the real finding and it was not raised at the time.

### P119 — Explicit marker distinguishing a composed artefact from a leaf artefact

- Theme: multi-org-ecosystem · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. compose.py labels and annotations, the render_is_faithful check, compose-check, ADR-0012 point 5, and any verifier reading the composed file.
- Then recommended: Yes - mark it explicitly, extending the spike's existing composed-for label and inherited-from/source-path annotations, so a verifier knows it must also check parent SHAs.
- Owner's words: Owner, 2026-08-20: 'there must never be an exemption ledger EVER, this is a banned concept explicitly, everything is codified' - a composed artefact that is silently indistinguishable from a leaf is an uncodified fact about how policy came to be. Owner, 2026-07-23, on provenance as a hard rule for machine-authored change. Owner, 2026-08-21, on composition as ordinary dependency inheritance - and every dependency system records that a thing was composed.
- **Now recommended:** Stands. One strengthening for the eco-system reading: the marker currently says 'this is composed', which is the weaker half. It should carry the parent set identity, so the marker alone answers 'composed from whom' - which is the question a regulator or a marketplace consumer actually asks. ADR-0012 point 6 already puts the parent-SHA record at the top of the file; make the marker and that record one thing.
- Rationale: Cheap, reuses metadata the spike already proved strips cleanly back to the file Kyverno reads, and the alternative (same shape either way) makes verification undecidable from the artefact alone. No convenience motive here in either direction.

### P120 — Who declares the compliance baseline - regulator, publisher, or adopter

- Theme: feeds-wargaming-and-marketplace · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Medium. ADR-0013, two CONTEXT.md terms, policy-composition tickets 03/09/14, the nist party artefact's OSCAL profiles, the adopter-side baseline selection field, the baseline coverage-and-holes gate, and the ac-6 / cm-6 straddle that the demo's baseline hole depends on.
- Then recommended: Split the question: the regulator publishes named baselines as versioned, signed OSCAL profiles; the adopter selects one by name, because selection is the risk-bearing act.
- Owner's words: Owner, 2026-08-19: 'we've developed a reference arch, and a platform but also a whole economic platform and model for risk feeds' - a regulator publishing versioned machine-readable baselines is precisely that feed. Owner, 2026-07-23, on exemptions: 'Exemptions are less an exemption. They are the bau. Ie you can do a thing under certain circumstances, if anyone in the org is able to meet the rules they can' - a named, universally available baseline is that, and the adopter's selection is the informed act. Owner, 2026-08-19: 'the financial risk of 21707 doesn't seem sane to the same on every org, its proporiate to the org right?' - which only works if each org selects its own baseline.
- **Now recommended:** Stands, and is the best decision in this batch for the eco-system north star: it makes a regulator's addition to a named baseline a real downstream build break in every org that selected it, which is the single most demonstrable moment the whole estate has. Extend it rather than revisit it - the selection should carry the £ consequence of a hole (ADR-0013 currently records who selects, not what a hole costs), so the adopter's choice of LOW over MODERATE shows up on the balance sheet.
- Rationale: One honesty note the owner should have: the three options put to him were regulator, publisher or adopter, and I answered with a fourth - the split - then took 'agree' as ratifying it. He agreed to something he was not offered. The reasoning holds independently (publisher-declares makes coverage tautological; regulator-declares cannot know the system; adopter-enumerates never sees a regulator's addition), which is why I still recommend re-ratifying rather than re-grilling - but the record should say the split was mine.

### P122 — Resolver behaviour on an unresolvable control id

- Theme: honesty-and-verification · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small if reversed — resolver in platform/compose/composition.py only. But reversing would silently re-open the class of bug the exact match exists to catch, so the blast radius understates the cost.
- Then recommended: Exact-string match against the catalogue, never case-fold or strip prefixes; an unmatched id is a hard failure with the id printed; the resolver must walk nested controls so enhancements like ac-6.10 are not missed.
- Owner's words: No quote on resolvers specifically, but the owner's standing posture is that silent success is the failure mode: 'show the real things, kubectl, failures' (2026-07-23), 'make sure the ticket isn't closed as its not done (or reopen it)' (2026-07-31), 'how on earth are you saying its working!?!!' (2026-08-25). A forgiving resolver is precisely the mechanism that turns a typo into a green run. Inferred from pattern, not stated on point.
- **Now recommended:** Unchanged. Keep exact-string with the failing id printed, and keep the nested walk. One addition against the north star: when the failure is a publisher-side id change rather than an adopter typo, the refusal should name the parent version and sha it resolved against, so the adopter can see it as a feed change, not their own error.
- Rationale: This is one of the few in the batch where the then-recommendation cost the assistant something rather than saving it work — a hard failure means more red runs, not fewer. It is the honest option, it survives the eco-system framing (provenance requires that ids mean exactly one thing), and it does not touch the gate/cage question at all.

### P125 — Where the adopter's baseline selection is recorded

- Theme: versioned-policy-dependency · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small. party.yaml and nist-pin-configmap.yaml in three repos, composition.py's reader, ADR-0013, ticket 11.
- Then recommended: The adopter's own party artefact — the input it gitsign-signs under ADR-0012. nist-pin-configmap.yaml gains a baselineName key and stays an advisory mirror; no new file is created.
- Owner's words: Consistent with the owner's provenance requirement — the arc records 'attestable provenance on every machine-authored commit' and 'gitsign-to-Rekor provenance' as stated ambition (2026-07-23) — and with his refusal of new stores that duplicate state. No quote directly on where a baseline selection lives.
- **Now recommended:** Unchanged. The signed party artefact is the right home; the configmap staying an advisory mirror is right, and the code already carries the comment saying so. If P124 is reversed and selection becomes per-adopter and priced, this is exactly where that price should be declared too.
- Rationale: Straightforwardly correct and already built (party.yaml `baseline: MODERATE`, configmap `baselineName` mirror). The signed input is the only copy an auditor can verify, and putting the selection anywhere else would create a second source of truth the estate has otherwise avoided. No demo or convenience pressure visible in the reasoning.

### P126 — Whether the adopter may tailor the selected baseline by removing controls

- Theme: no-exemptions-all-policy · Alignment: **aligned** · Blast radius if reversed: Small to reverse in code (composer would accept a remove list), very large in doctrine — it would re-introduce the concept the owner banned by name, and would contradict ADR-0013 and the cage story the deck tells.
- Then recommended: Add only. Removal is an exemption by another name; a control the adopter cannot meet goes to the cage and is priced, never removed from the baseline.
